## Page 1


TILT-STABILITY ON SINGULAR SCHEMES AND
BOGOMOLOV–GIESEKER-TYPE INEQUALITIES
ZHIYU LIU AND TIANLE MAO
Abstract. We generalize the framework of tilt-stability to singular schemes and formulate the gener-
alized Bogomolov–Gieseker inequality conjecture of Bayer–Macr`ı–Toda for singular threefolds. We also
develop relative versions of these constructions, generalizing corresponding results in [BLM+21]. Along
the way, we establish Bogomolov–Gieseker-type inequalities for semistable sheaves on any projective
scheme.
By extending previous techniques, we verify the conjecture for all Fano threefolds with canonical
Gorenstein Q-factorial singularities and a series of singular Calabi–Yau threefolds.
Furthermore, we
construct stability conditions on the relative Kuznetsov components associated with families of singular
Fano threefolds, thereby proving a singular analogue of a conjecture of Kuznetsov–Shinder.

1. Introduction
Motivated by Douglas’s work on Π-stability, Bridgeland introduces the notion of stability conditions on
triangulated categories [Bri07]. More recently, Li [Li26] proves the existence of stability conditions on the
derived categories of projective schemes, while the relative version and the properness of the corresponding
moduli spaces are established in [LLL+26].
For many geometric applications, it is more effective to work with tilt-stability rather than full stability
conditions. The construction of tilt-stability is introduced by [Bri08, AB13], which provides examples of
stability conditions on surfaces. Later, it is extended to any smooth projective variety in [BMT14, BMS16].
Although tilt-stability is only a weak stability condition on higher-dimensional varieties (cf. Definition 4.2),
it provides a powerful and computable tool to study sheaves on varieties and has many applications, in-
cluding birational geometry of moduli spaces of sheaves [BM14b, AB13], hyper-K¨ahler geometry [BM14a],
Brill–Noether theory of curves [Bay18, FL21, BL17], effective restriction problems [Fey22, Fey20], etc.
2020 Mathematics Subject Classification. 14F08 (Primary); 14F06, 14D06, 14D20 (Secondary).
Key words and phrases. Derived categories, Tilt-stability, Generalized Bogomolov–Gieseker inequalities, Stability
conditions.

ZHIYU LIU AND TIANLE MAO
Moreover, Bayer–Macr`ı–Toda [BMT14, BMS16] introduced a conjectural Bogomolov–Gieseker-type
inequality involving ch3 of tilt-stable objects. Although originally proposed as a route to construct stability
conditions on smooth threefolds, such a ch3-inequality also plays a crucial role in enumerative geometry of
Calabi–Yau threefolds [Tod13a, FT23a, FT24, FT23b, AFK+24, LR22, Liu24] and in birational geometry
[BBMT14].
On the other hand, singularities arise naturally even when one is ultimately interested only in smooth
varieties. For instance, it is often useful to study degenerations of smooth varieties to singular schemes with
richer geometric structures. Motivated by degeneration techniques in enumerative geometry (cf. [PP17]),
it is natural to expect that Bayer–Macr`ı–Toda’s (BMT) Conjecture might be approached through such a
method.
Singular varieties are also unavoidable in birational geometry.
A conjectural relation between the
minimal model program for smooth threefolds and stability conditions is proposed in [Tod13b, Tod14].
Since the threefolds appearing in the program are terminal and Q-factorial, it is therefore natural to
investigate tilt-stability and the BMT Conjecture for these singular threefolds. We refer to Section 1.4
for a more detailed discussion of these motivations and related questions.
To generalize the theory above to singular varieties and pursue applications, the first step is to develop
a theory of tilt-stability in the singular setting. The main results of this paper can be summarized as
follows.
• We extend tilt-stability to singular schemes, formulate the BMT Conjecture in this setting, and
develop relative versions of these constructions, establishing their basic properties.
• We verify the BMT Conjecture for singular Fano threefolds and a series of singular Calabi–Yau
threefolds.
• We prove a semistable reduction theorem for tilt-semistable objects, showing that the BMT
Conjecture can be verified via degeneration.
• We construct stability conditions on relative Kuznetsov components associated with families of
singular Fano threefolds.

For smooth projective varieties and normal projective surfaces over any field, Theorem 1.1(a) is proved

Remark 1.2. Parts (b) and (c) of Theorem 1.1 follow from more general theorems for tilting of abstract
weak stability conditions on triangulated categories, see Theorem 6.1 and 6.24. In the geometric setting,
tilt-stability satisfies many expected properties as listed in Section 9.3 and 9.4.
Note that Theorem 1.1(b) and (c) also hold for normal projective surfaces. In this case, ch(−), the
corresponding Bogomolov–Gieseker inequality, and tilt-stability have already been established in [Lan24b].
Remark 1.3. In Appendix A, we present an alternative treatment of tilt-stability on arbitrary projective
schemes. Rather than using the Chern characters chi(−), this approach defines tilt-stability using the
(normalized) coefficients of the Hilbert polynomial. Furthermore, we establish an analogue of Theorem
1.1 within this framework. Specifically, we prove a BG-type inequality involving the first three coefficients
of the Hilbert polynomial for semistable sheaves on any projective scheme (cf. Theorem A.1), which may
be of independent interest.
However, the primary focus of this paper is the BMT Conjecture, which requires a sharper Bogomolov-
Gieseker-type inequality and a well-behaved notion of ch3, so we do not explore Appendix A further in
the main text.

Remark 1.5. As discussed above, the constant D in the formulation always exists, and should be viewed
as a correction term to the classical Bogomolov–Gieseker inequality for µH-semistable sheaves, reflecting
singularities of X and char(k).
Here, E(X)Q denotes the bivariant Chow group A2(X)Q of degree 2 when X is lci, or CH1(X)Q when
X is Q-factorial but not lci. In particular, we always have an intersection pairing E(X)Q ×CH2(X)Q →Q.
As in the smooth case, the above conjecture has two equivalent formulations; see Conjecture 10.1 and
10.5. Moreover, by Theorem 10.7, Conjecture 1.4 allows us to construct an explicit family of stability
conditions on X, which generalizes the corresponding results in [BMS16, BMSZ17].
Conjecture 1.4 has been proved for many smooth threefolds, including smooth Fano threefolds [Li19b,
BMSZ17], smooth Calabi–Yau complete intersections in weighted projective spaces [Li19a, FKLR25,
Kos22, Liu22], and a series of smooth Calabi–Yau threefolds [FKLR25]. Moreover, by [FKLR25], Conjec-
ture 1.4 for smooth Calabi–Yau threefolds can be reduced to a conjectural inequality for ch2 for µH-stable
sheaves with small slope. We extend this reduction method to a more general setting in Section 11.1 and
apply it to a broad class of singular threefolds.

We also generalize criteria in [FKLR25, Theorem 3.1]:
Theorem 1.8 (Theorem 11.13). Let (X, H) be a polarised normal projective threefold over k with rational
Gorenstein singularities, such that KX is numerically trivial, H1(OX) = 0, and either X is lci or Q-
factorial. Fix divisors S ∈|H| and C ∈|H|S|. Assume either

Example 1.9. In addition to the singular analogs of smooth examples considered in previous papers, we
also apply Theorem 11.13 to certain singular Calabi–Yau threefolds that do not admit smoothings. An
example is a general degree 8 hypersurface in the weighted projective space P(1, 1, 1, 2, 3) (cf. Corollary
11.16).
1.3. Tilt-stability in the relative setting. In the spirit of [BLM+21, PT19, Tod08], we also show that
tilt-stability behaves well in a flat family (cf. Theorem 9.25), which generalizes [BLM+21, Theorem 25.3].
In particular, for a suitable flat projective family of 2-dimensional schemes in characteristic 0, the moduli
stacks of tilt-semistable objects admit good moduli spaces that are proper over the base, generalizing
results in [Tod08].
Theorem 1.10 (Corollary 9.28, absolute version). Let X be a pure 2-dimensional projective scheme over
a field k of characteristic 0 that is either geometrically normal or lci. Then for any w > ΦX,H(b), the
moduli stack of νb,w-semistable objects with a fixed class (chi(−).H2−i)0≤i≤2 is an Artin stack of finite
type over k, and admits a good moduli space which is proper over k.

1.4. Stability conditions on singular Kuznetsov components. For many Fano manifolds, the de-
rived category contains distinguished semi-orthogonal components, called Kuznetsov components. These
subcategories have been studied extensively; see, for example, [Kuz06, Kuz10, Kuz19, Kuz09, Kuz04,
KP18].
Using a rotation of tilt-stability, stability conditions on a series of Fano manifolds are constructed in
[BLMS23], which is later generalized to the relative setting by [BLM+21, Section 26]. Such a construction
is applied to the moduli theory of sheaves and the geometry of Fano threefolds in [BMMS12, PY22, LZ22,
JLLZ24, JLZ22, FLZ24, JLLZ23, FP23], and the study of hyper-K¨ahler manifolds in [BLM+21, PPZ22,
SY20, LPZ23, LPZ22, GLZ24, FGLZ25b, FGLZ25a, GL25].
On the other hand, Kuznetsov components can also be defined for many singular Fano varieties. In
Theorem 12.7, 12.8, and 12.9, we generalize results in [BLMS23, Section 6] and [BLM+21, Corollary 26.2]
to Kuznetsov components of singular Fano threefolds.
As an application, we settle a singular variant of a conjecture of Kuznetsov–Shinder [KS25, Conjecture
1.8].
More precisely, for each 1 ≤d ≤5, [KS25, Theorem 3.6] constructs a family X →B of Fano
threefolds such that B is a smooth complex curve, Xo is a 1-nodal index 1 Fano threefold of genus 2d + 2
for a closed point o ∈B, and Xb is a smooth index 1 Fano threefold of genus 2d + 2 for each b ∈B \ {o}.
Moreover, there exists a smooth proper category ¯
AX ⊂Db(X) over B whose fiber over b ∈B \ {o} is the
Kuznetsov component of Xb, while the fiber over o is equivalent to the Kuznetsov component of a smooth
index 2 Fano threefold of degree d.
In [KS25, Conjecture 1.8], it is expected that ¯
AX carries a stability condition over B. When B is the
spectrum of a complete DVR, this is proved by [LMP+26]. Using tilt-stability, we prove an analog of this
conjecture for a slightly larger semi-orthogonal component AX/B ⊂Db(X), which differs from ¯
AX only
over o ∈B.
Corollary 1.12 (Corollary 12.10). In the above setting, if d ≥2, then there exists a stability condition
on AX/B over B.
Since the orthogonal complement of ¯
AX in AX/B is generated by an explicit object (cf. [KS25, Theorem
3.6]), we expect that Corollary 12.10 can be used to induce stability conditions on ¯
AX , hence to prove
[KS25, Conjecture 1.8] in full generality.
Motivations and further questions. Here, we discuss some motivations and problems that are closely
related to this paper.
BMT Conjecture for more threefolds. A fundamental technique in algebraic geometry is degeneration.
Concretely, one considers a family X →B together with two points 0, 1 ∈B such that the fiber X0 is the
variety of interest, while X1 has richer geometry. This method is widely used in Gromov–Witten theory,


## Page 6


6
ZHIYU LIU AND TIANLE MAO
for example, in the proof of the MNOP Conjecture [PP17]. A key step is to establish an appropriate
degeneration formula for the problem under consideration.
Motivated by this perspective, a main motivation for developing tilt-stability on singular schemes is
to study the behavior of Conjecture 1.4 under degeneration. By applying Theorem 10.12 to a suitable
degeneration, we expect that Conjecture 1.4 can be verified for many smooth threefolds. Indeed, many
such threefolds admit degenerations to unions of smooth threefolds for which Conjecture 1.4 is already
known, such as Fano threefolds. For instance, let X be a smooth Calabi–Yau threefold given by the
complete intersection of a quadric hypersurface and a codimension-4 linear section of Gr(2, 6). Then X
admits a degeneration to a union X′ of two codimension-5 linear sections of Gr(2, 6), each of which is
a smooth Fano threefold of Picard number one. A Bogomolov–Gieseker-type inequality for semistable
sheaves on X′ is proved in Theorem 8.4. Consequently, the framework of this paper applies to X′. It
is therefore natural to expect that Conjecture 1.4 can be proved for such a union by combining the
corresponding results for its Fano components, and that the conjecture for X would then follow from
Theorem 10.12. In the spirit of [LMP+26], one may also consider the logarithmic derived category on the
singular fiber; see [DHKH+26a, DHKH+26b].
We also note that our result for singular Fano threefolds in Corollary 11.9 is not expected to be optimal.
For smooth Fano threefolds of Picard number one, Conjecture 10.2 with Γ = 0 is proved in [Li19b]. For
the higher Picard rank case, an effective choice of Γ is obtained in [BMSZ17]. We expect that a more
explicit version of Corollary 11.9 can be established by adapting the methods of [Li19b, BMSZ17]. Similar
ideas may also apply to weak Fano threefolds.
Birational geometry. Singularities arise naturally in the minimal model program. It is conjectured in
[Tod13b, Question 1.1] that each step of the minimal model program for a smooth threefold can be
realized as a wall-crossing of moduli spaces of stable objects. This has been established in dimension 2
in [Tod14], and partial progress in dimension 3 is made in [Tod13b]. In particular, using the perverse
t-structure on Db(X) associated with a birational contraction X →Y , [Tod13b] introduces a perverse
version of tilt-stability and a corresponding form of Conjecture 1.4 for X. Note that every threefold
arising in the minimal model program of a smooth threefold has terminal Q-factorial singularities, so it
fits perfectly into our framework. It is therefore natural to generalize the constructions of [Tod13b] to
singular threefolds and to compare the resulting perverse theory with the theory developed in this paper.
We expect that such a comparison could lead to a solution of [Tod13b, Question 1.1] in dimension 3.
On the other hand, effective basepoint-freeness results play a central role in birational geometry. For a
smooth threefold X, it is proved in [BBMT14] that Conjecture 1.4 implies an effective generation theorem
for adjoint linear series, which in turn yields a version of Fujita’s conjecture. It is therefore an interesting
problem to extend these results to singular threefolds.
Curve-counting theory. Another natural source of singular threefolds is provided by quotients by finite
groups, and more generally by coarse moduli spaces of Deligne–Mumford stacks. The crepant resolution
conjecture relates the curve-counting invariants of a smooth 3-dimensional Deligne–Mumford stack X to
those of a crepant resolution Y of its coarse moduli space X. In the context of Donaldson–Thomas theory,
the invariants of a smooth Calabi–Yau 3-dimensional stack X are related to those of Y in [BCR22]. As
explained in [BCR22, Section 2.2], the coarse moduli space X has Gorenstein quotient singularities, and
therefore has a well-behaved theory of tilt-stability. On the other hand, wall-crossing for tilt-stability and
Conjecture 1.4 have been used to study Donaldson–Thomas invariants in [Tod13a, FT24, FT23b, FT23a,
LR22]. It is therefore natural to expect that the relationship between the Donaldson–Thomas invariants
of X and Y can be understood through the wall-crossing behavior of tilt-stability on the coarse moduli
space X.
A different approach to using Conjecture 1.4 to study curves is developed in [MS20, LR22, AFK+24,
Liu24]. In particular, Conjecture 1.4 yields various bounds on the genus of curves in threefolds, which in
turn imply vanishing theorems for curve-counting invariants of smooth Calabi–Yau threefolds. Using the
framework of this paper, we expect that these results can also be extended to the singular setting.


## Page 7


TILT-STABILITY ON SINGULAR SCHEMES AND BG-TYPE INEQUALITIES
7
Study of moduli spaces. Moduli spaces of semistable objects are among the main geometric outputs of
stability conditions. In the smooth setting, stability conditions on Kuznetsov components have led to
the construction and study of many remarkable moduli spaces, including hyper-K¨ahler manifolds and
their birational models, and moduli of sheaves on smooth Fano threefolds. Since we construct stability
conditions on relative Kuznetsov components associated with singular families in Section 12, it is natural to
ask how the corresponding moduli spaces behave in degenerating families, especially using Corollary 1.12.
In particular, one expects moduli spaces on smooth Kuznetsov components to admit natural degenerations
to moduli spaces associated with the singular fibers. Such a picture would provide a categorical approach
to studying degenerations of hyper-K¨ahler manifolds and moduli spaces of sheaves on Fano varieties.
On the other hand, wall-crossing for stability gives a powerful description of the birational geometry of
moduli spaces on smooth K3 surfaces and related hyper-K¨ahler manifolds [BM14a, BM14b]. It is therefore
natural to seek an analogous picture for moduli spaces on singular symplectic surfaces. One may then
hope to use the resulting wall-crossing to study the minimal model program for the corresponding moduli
spaces and, more generally, to extend the results of [BM14a, BM14b] from hyper-K¨ahler manifolds to
singular hyper-K¨ahler varieties.
Organization. In Section 2, we review preliminaries on derived categories and singular schemes. Then
Section 3 develops the intersection-theoretic and Chern character formalism needed in the singular setting.
Sections 4 and 5 recall stability conditions and their relative versions in families. In Section 6, we prove
an abstract tilting theorem for weak stability conditions (cf. Theorem 6.1) and establish its wall-chamber
structure in Theorem 6.24.
In Section 7, we first introduce the necessary notation, then we study slope-stability and its relative
version in Sections 7.1 and 7.2.
In Section 8, we first define the Le Potier function in the absolute and relative settings. In Theorem
8.4, we show that it is always bounded above by a quadratic function, and in Theorem 8.6 we sharpen
this bound for normal varieties.
Section 9 focuses on tilt-stability constructed from slope-stability. We begin with a rotation of slope-
stability and its relative version in Proposition 9.6. We then establish the basic properties and useful
lemmas for tilt-stability in Sections 9.3 and 9.4. The main result on tilt-stability in families is Theorem
9.25.
In Section 10, we first formulate the BMT Conjecture on projective threefolds and prove that it is
equivalent to two other weaker formulations (cf. Theorem 10.6). Using these conjectures, Theorem 10.7
shows that we can construct an explicit family of stability conditions. We end this section by establishing
a semistable reduction result, Theorem 10.10, for tilt-semistable objects, which allows us to check the
BMT Conjecture via degeneration (cf. Theorem 10.12).
In Section 11, we first generalize the reduction method in [FKLR25] to a more general setting in
Theorem 11.5. Using this, we verify the BMT Conjecture for Fano threefolds and a series of Calabi–Yau
threefolds. Moreover, a singular Calabi–Yau threefold without smoothing is considered in Corollary 11.16.
In Section 12, we generalize [BLM+21, Theorem 23.1] to families of singular schemes.
Using this,
we construct stability conditions on Kuznetsov components associated with singular Fano threefolds. A
singular variant of Kuznetsov–Shinder’s conjecture [KS25, Conjecture 1.8] is proved in Corollary 12.10.
Finally, in Appendix A, we discuss how to replace the Chern characters in the construction of tilt-
stability with coefficients of Hilbert polynomials, and prove an analog of Theorem 1.1. In particular, a
Bogomolov–Gieseker-type inequality for semistable sheaves on an arbitrary projective scheme is estab-
lished in Theorem A.1.

2. Preliminaries
In this section, we collect preliminaries on derived categories, singular schemes, and relative objects
used later.
2.1. Notations and conventions. We begin by summarizing some basic notions.
• Given a scheme X, a point t →X means a morphism from the spectrum of a field, and it is
called a geometric point if the corresponding field is algebraically closed. We write t ∈X when
t →X identifies the source field with the residue field of its image.
• We say a locally Noetherian scheme X is equidimensional if each irreducible component of X
has the same finite Krull dimension and there are no embedded components.
• A Dedekind scheme is an integral, Noetherian, one-dimensional regular scheme. For a Dedekind
scheme C, we write p ∈C for a closed point, c ∈C for an arbitrary point, η ∈C for the generic
point, and K for its fraction field.
• We say a morphism f : X →Y between schemes is essentially of finite type if f is either of finite
type, or X is affine and f factors as Spec(S−1A) →Spec(A) →Y , where Spec(A) →Y is a
morphism of finite type and S−1A is a localization of A.
• A Nagata scheme is a scheme X such that we have an affine open cover S
i∈I Spec(Ai) = X with
each Ai a Nagata ring in the sense of [Sta25, Tag 032R]. The Nagata property is preserved under
morphisms essentially of finite type by [Sta25, Tag 032U] and [Sta25, Tag 0334]. Moreover, the
normalization morphism of X is finite by [Sta25, Tag 035S].
• A morphism X →Y between schemes is embeddable if it can be written as a composition
X ,→P →Y , where P →Y is a smooth morphism and X ,→P is a closed embedding. In this
case, we also say that X is embeddable over Y .
• A variety over a field k is a geometrically integral scheme of finite type over k. A threefold over
k is a variety over k of dimension 3.
• For a normal variety X over a field of characteristic 0, we say X has rational singularities if
the natural map OX →Rf∗OY is an isomorphism for a (hence any) resolution of singularities
f : Y →X. By [KM98, Theorem 4.20, Corollary 5.24], if X has rational Gorenstein singularities
and dim X = 2, then X is a local complete intersection.
• We denote by K(D) the K-group of a triangulated category or an abelian category D.
• For a lattice Λ and any subset S ⊂Λ, we denote by ⟨S⟩the saturation of the subgroup generated
by S in Λ. For any Z-module A, we denote by AF := A ⊗Z F, where F is any field extension of Q.
2.2. Derived categories. For a scheme X, we consider the following derived categories:
• the unbounded derived category D(X) of sheaves of OX-modules; for any object E ∈D(X), we
denote by Hi(E) ∈Mod(OX) the i-th cohomology sheaf,
• the derived category Dqc(X) (resp. D+
qc(X), D−
qc(X)) of OX-modules with (resp. bounded below,
bounded above) quasi-coherent cohomology sheaves,
• the category Dpc(X) of pseudo-coherent complexes; here, we say a complex in D(X) is pseudo-
coherent if affine locally, it is quasi-isomorphic to a bounded above complex of finitely generated
locally free sheaves,
• the derived category Db(X) of pseudo-coherent complexes on X with bounded cohomology
sheaves,
• when X is Noetherian, the derived categories D+(X) and D−(X) of OX-modules with bounded
below and bounded above coherent cohomology sheaves, respectively; and
• the full triangulated subcategory Dperf(X) ⊂D(X) of perfect complexes on X. If X is Noether-
ian, we have Dperf(X) ⊂Db(X). When X is regular and quasi-compact, we have Dperf(X) =
Db(X) (cf. [Sta25, Tag 0FDC]).

According to [Sta25, Tag 08E8], if X is Noetherian, then Dpc(X) = D−(X) coincides with the bounded
above derived category of OX-modules with coherent cohomology sheaves. In this case, we also have
Db(X) = Db(Coh(X)), D−(X) = D−(Coh(X)), and Dqc(X) = D(QCoh(X)).
We denote by K0(X) and K0(X) the K-group of Db(X) and Dperf(X), respectively.
For a morphism between schemes f : X →Y , we have the following derived functors:
• the derived pushforward
Rf∗: Dqc(X) →Dqc(Y )
when f is quasi-compact and quasi-separated. It induces a functor
Rf∗: Db(X) →Db(Y )
when f is proper and Y is Noetherian. If f is furthermore perfect in the sense of [Sta25, Tag
0687], e.g. Y is regular (cf. [Sta25, Tag 068B]), or f is flat and locally of finite presentation
(cf. [Sta25, Tag 068A]), or f is a local complete intersection morphism (cf. [Sta25, Tag 069H]),
then it restricts to
Rf∗: Dperf(X) →Dperf(Y )
by [Sta25, Tag 0B6G],
• the derived pullback Lf∗: Dqc(Y ) →Dqc(X) and Lf ∗: Dperf(Y ) →Dperf(X). If f is perfect,
then it restricts to
Lf ∗: Db(Y ) →Db(X),
and
• the upper shriek f ! : D+
qc(Y ) →D+
qc(X) and f ! : D+(Y ) →D+(X) if Y is Noetherian and f
is separated and of finite type (cf. [Sta25, Tag 0AA0] and [Sta25, Tag 0AU1]).
If f is also
proper, then f ! is the right adjoint of Rf∗(cf. [Sta25, Tag 0F42]). If f is perfect, then it induces
f ! : Db(Y ) →Db(X) by [Sta25, Tag 0B6U]. If f is a local complete intersection morphism, then
it induces
f ! : Dperf(Y ) →Dperf(X)
by [Sta25, Tag 0B6V].
We also have functors
RHomX(−, −): D(X)op × D(X) →D(X)
and
−⊗L −: D(X) × D(X) →D(X).
When X is locally Noetherian, they restrict to functors D−(X)op × D+(X) →D+(X) and D−(X) ×
D−(X) →D−(X), respectively. By [Sta25, Tag 08DJ], for any E, F, G ∈D(X), we have
RHomX(E, RHomX(F, G)) ∼= RHomX(E ⊗L F, G).
When Y is Noetherian and has the resolution property in the sense of [Sta25, Tag 0F86], any object in
Dperf(Y ) is quasi-isomorphic to a bounded complex of finite locally free sheaves on Y and any object in
D−(Y ) is quasi-isomorphic to a bounded above complex of finite locally free sheaves on Y . In this case,
if f : X →Y is a morphism from another Noetherian scheme X, then we have
Lf ∗RHomY (E, F) ∼= RHomX(Lf ∗E, Lf ∗F)
for any E ∈D−(Y ) and F ∈D+(Y ) if either E ∈Dperf(Y ) or f has finite Tor-dimension (cf. [Sta25, Tag
0GM7]).
For a locally Noetherian scheme X and an object E ∈D−(X), we define the derived dual
DX(E) := RHomX(E, OX) ∈D+(X)
and its shift DX
n (E) := DX(E)[n], and the underived dual
E∨= HomX(E, OX) := H0(DX(E)) ∈Coh(X).
If X = Spec(k) for a field k, then E is a complex of finite-dimensional k-vector spaces, and we write
E∗:= DX(E) to match the usual notation of dual vector spaces.

2.3. Semi-orthogonal decompositions. We now review semi-orthogonal decompositions in both ab-
solute and relative settings. We refer to [BLM+21, Section 3] for a more detailed introduction. Let D be
a triangulated category.
Definition 2.1. A semi-orthogonal decomposition of D is a sequence of full triangulated subcategories
D1, . . . , Dm of D, called the components of the decomposition, such that
D = ⟨D1, . . . , Dm⟩,
where ⟨−⟩denotes the extension closure, satisfying
• HomD(Di, Dj) = 0 for i > j, and
• for any E ∈D, there is a sequence of morphisms
0 = Em →Em−1 →· · · →E0 = E
such that pri(E) := cone(Ei →Ei−1) ∈Di for each 1 ≤i ≤m.
Then we obtain a functor pri : D →Di ,→D for each 1 ≤i ≤m, which is called the projection functor
onto Di.
Definition 2.2. A semi-orthogonal decomposition D = ⟨D1, . . . , Dm⟩is called strong if for each i the
inclusion functor Di ,→D has a right adjoint. A full subcategory D′ ⊂D is a strong semi-orthogonal
component if it is part of a strong semi-orthogonal decomposition of D.
Definition 2.3. Let X be a Noetherian scheme. We say a semi-orthogonal decomposition D = ⟨D1, . . . , Dm⟩
of a triangulated subcategory D ⊂Dqc(X) is of finite cohomological amplitude if there exists p, q ∈Z such
that the projection functor pri onto each component Di satisfies
pri(D ∩D[a,b]
qc (X)) ⊂D[a+p,b+q]
qc
(X)
for all a, b ∈Z, where DI
qc(X) denotes the full subcategory of Dqc(X) consisting of objects whose coho-
mology sheaves are concentrated in degrees in I ⊂Z.
We say a semi-orthogonal component D of Dperf(X), Db(X), or Dqc(X) is of finite cohomological
amplitude if the semi-orthogonal decomposition defining D is of finite cohomological amplitude.
By [BLM+21, Lemma 3.10, 3.13], if X is Noetherian, then any semi-orthogonal component D ⊂Db(X)
induces semi-orthogonal components Dperf ⊂Dperf(X) and Dqc ⊂Dqc(X).
We have the following definition when we work over a general base scheme.
Definition 2.4. Let f : X →S be a morphism between schemes. A triangulated subcategory D ⊂Dqc(X)
is called S-linear if for any E ∈D and F ∈Dperf(S), one has E ⊗L Lf ∗F ∈D. A semi-orthogonal
decomposition of D is called S-linear if all of its components are S-linear.
2.4. Serre’s conditions for sheaves and morphisms. Let X be a locally Noetherian scheme. Recall
that the dimension and codimension of a coherent sheaf E on X are the dimension and codimension
of its support Supp(E), which are denoted by dim(E) and codimX(E), respectively. We say E is pure
of dimension d if dim(F) = d for any non-zero subsheaf F ⊂E. This is equivalent to saying that all
associated points of E have the same dimension.
We say E is a torsion sheaf if Supp(E) is nowhere dense in X, or equivalently, the set Ass(E) of
associated points of E does not contain any generic point of X. For any E ∈Coh(X), there is a largest

11
torsion subsheaf tor(E) ⊂E, which is called the torsion part of E. We say E is torsion-free if tor(E) = 0,
or equivalently, every point in Ass(E) is a generic point of X. If X is equidimensional, it follows from the
definition that a non-zero sheaf E is torsion-free if and only if E is pure of dimension dim(X).
Remark 2.5. In some papers, torsion-freeness is only defined for sheaves on integral schemes. It is clear
that when X is integral, the two definitions coincide; see for example, [Sta25, Tag 0AUV].
Now we review Serre’s conditions Sn.
Recall that for a coherent sheaf E on a locally Noetherian
scheme X, the depth depthx(E) at a point x ∈X is defined as the depth of E|x as a finite OX,x-module
(cf. [Sta25, Tag 00LI]).
Definition 2.6 ([Sta25, Tag 0341]). Let X be a locally Noetherian scheme, E ∈Coh(X), and n ∈Z≥0.
We say E is Sn if
depthx(E) ≥min{n, dim(E|x)}
for any x ∈X. We say X is Sn if OX is. We say E is Cohen–Macaulay if it is Sn for all n ≥0.
In particular, E is S1 if and only if it has no embedded points (cf. [Sta25, Tag 0346]). Therefore, E is
pure if and only if Supp(E) is equidimensional and E is S1. If X is normal and integral, then torsion-free
S2 sheaves on X coincide with reflexive sheaves by [Sta25, Tag 0AVB]. In the following, we will see that
torsion-free S2 sheaves share many useful properties with reflexive sheaves.
Lemma 2.7. Let X be a locally Noetherian scheme and E be a coherent sheaf on X.
(a) If F ∈Coh(X), then Ass(HomX(E, F)) ⊂Ass(F). In particular, if F is pure of dimension d
(resp. torsion-free), then HomX(E, F) is also pure of dimension d (resp. torsion-free).
(b) If F is an S2 sheaf on X, then HomX(E, F) is also S2.
(c) If E is torsion-free and S2, then the natural map E →j∗j∗E is an isomorphism for any open
embedding j : U ,→X with codimX(X \ j(U)) ≥2.
(d) If E is torsion-free and S2, then for any T ∈Coh(X) with codimX(T) ≥2, we have
HomX(T, E[1]) = 0.
Proof. Affine locally, we can take a surjection O⊕n
X
↠E, which gives an inclusion HomX(E, F) ⊂F ⊕n
and part (a) follows. Part (b) is [Sta25, Tag 0AXQ] and part (c) is [Sta25, Tag 0E9I].
For part (d), let U := X \ Supp(T) and j : U ,→X be the inclusion. Let F be the extension of T
and E corresponds to an element of HomX(T, E[1]). If E is S2, then j∗j∗F ∼= j∗j∗E ∼= E by part (c).
Therefore, the composition of natural maps E ,→F →j∗j∗F ∼= j∗j∗E is an isomorphism. Thus, F splits
and HomX(T, E[1]) = 0 follows.
□
We also have the following generalization of Sn for morphisms:
Definition 2.8 ([Sta25, Tag 045R]). Let f : X →Y be a morphism between schemes such that each fiber
Xy is locally Noetherian. We say f is Sn if f is flat and every fiber of f is Sn. We say f is Cohen–Macaulay
if f is Sn for each n ≥0. We say f is Gorenstein if f is flat and every fiber of f is Gorenstein.
By [Sta25, Tag 045U] and [Sta25, Tag 0E0Q], Cohen–Macaulay morphisms and Gorenstein morphisms
are stable under arbitrary base change. More generally, Sn morphisms are also stable under base change
by [Gro65, Proposition 6.7.1].
We will also use the notion of local complete intersection (lci) morphisms as in [Sta25, Tag 069F]. By
[Sta25, Tag 068E], the lci property is stable under composition and flat base change. By [Avr99], a flat
morphism f of finite type between Noetherian schemes is lci if and only if the cotangent complex Lf of
f is perfect with tor-amplitude in [−1, 0].
We say a morphism is syntomic if it is flat and lci (cf. [Sta25, Tag 069K]). By [Sta25, Tag 01UB], being
syntomic is stable under compositions and arbitrary base changes. If f is locally of finite presentation,
then f is syntomic if and only if f is flat and each fiber of f is lci.

with codimXy(Zy) ≥d + 1 such that Xy \ Zy is lci over κ(y) for each y ∈Y . If f is flat and locally of
finite presentation, then by [Sta25, Tag 06B8] or [Sta25, Tag 02V3],
LCI(X/Y ) := {x ∈X : Xf(x) is lci over κ(f(x)) at x}
is open in X and
LCI(XT /T) = g−1(LCI(X/Y ))

for any morphism T →Y , where g: XT →X is the induced morphism. In this case, f is fiberwise lci in
codimension d if and only if (X \ LCI(X/Y ))y = Xy \ LCI(Xy/κ(y)) has codimension ≥d + 1 in Xy for
each y ∈Y . If Y is the spectrum of a field k, then we simply say X is lci in codimension d (over k).
2.5. Relative perfect objects. Next, we recall the definition of relative perfect objects, which can be
viewed as a notion of families of bounded complexes of coherent sheaves over a base.
Definition 2.9 ([Sta25, Tag 0DI0]). Let f : X →S be a morphism which is flat and locally of finite
presentation. An object E ∈Dqc(X) is perfect relative to S, or S-perfect, if E is pseudo-coherent and
locally of finite Tor-dimension over f −1 OS.
Note that if S = Spec k for a field k and X is Noetherian, then E ∈Dqc(X) is S-perfect if and only if
E ∈Db(X). More generally, we have the following useful lemma.
Lemma 2.10. Let f : X →S be a morphism which is flat and locally of finite presentation.
(a) If E ∈Dqc(X) is S-perfect and X is quasi-compact, then E ∈Db(X).
(b) If E ∈Dperf(X), then E is S-perfect.
(c) If S is regular of finite dimension, then any object E ∈Db(X) is S-perfect.
(d) If f is smooth, then any S-perfect object E is perfect.
Proof. Parts (a)-(c) are included in [BLM+21, Lemma 8.3].
Part (d) is already mentioned in [Lie06, Section 2.1]. For a smooth morphism, OX is perfect over
f −1OS, and a pseudo-coherent object on X of finite Tor-amplitude over S has finite Tor-amplitude over
X. Hence E is perfect. Equivalently, this is [Sta25, Tag 09PC].
□
One of the advantages of working with relative perfect objects is that they behave well under base
change, proper pushforward, and derived internal hom.
Lemma 2.11. Let f : X →S be a morphism which is flat and locally of finite presentation and E ∈
Dqc(X) be a S-perfect object.
(a) If f is proper, then Rf∗E ∈Dperf(S) and its formation commutes with arbitrary base change.
(b) If S′ →S is another morphism, then ES′ ∈Dqc(XS′) is S′-perfect.
(c) If F ∈Dperf(X), then E ⊗L F is also S-perfect.
Proof. Part (a) is [Sta25, Tag 0DJT], part (b) is [Sta25, Tag 0DI5], and part (c) is [Sta25, Tag 0DI4].
□
We also have the following flattening stratification of relative perfect objects.
Lemma 2.12. Let X →S be a flat projective morphism between Noetherian schemes and E ∈Dqc(X)
be an S-perfect object. Assume that s0 ∈S is a point such that Hi(Es0) = 0 for i /∈[a, b], where a ≤b
and a, b ∈Z.
(a) There is an open neighborhood U ⊂S of s0 such that Hi(EU) = 0 and Hi(Es) = 0 for all i /∈[a, b]
and s ∈U.
(b) If Hi(Es) = 0 for i /∈[a, b] and for all s ∈S, then we can find a finite set of locally closed
subschemes {Sj}j∈J of S so that Hi(ESj) = 0 for i /∈[a, b], Hi(ESj) is flat over Sj for i ∈[a, b],
and S
j∈J Sj = S as a set.

Lemma 2.14. Let f : X →S be a Gorenstein separated morphism between locally Noetherian schemes

2.7. Duals and hulls of coherent sheaves. We now study Ext sheaves and hulls of coherent sheaves.
For any coherent sheaf E on an equidimensional Noetherian scheme X that admits a dualizing complex
ω•
X, we define
EH := HomX(HomX(E, ωX), ωX).
This is called the hull of E, and has a natural map qE : E →EH. The hull EH plays the same role in our
paper as reflexive hulls for sheaves on normal schemes. Note that if X is Gorenstein, then
EH = E∨∨= HomX(HomX(E, OX), OX).

3. Intersection theory and Chern characters
In this section, we first review basic facts about intersection theory over a general base scheme, then
we define and study Chern characters of bounded complexes of coherent sheaves on general schemes. We
closely follow the treatment of Chow groups and bivariant Chow groups in [Sta25, Tag 02P3].
3.1. Basic notions. We fix a locally Noetherian and universally catenary scheme S and a dimension
function δ: S →Z, i.e. we fix (S, δ) as in [Sta25, Tag 02QL].
For a morphism f : X →S of locally finite type, the δ-dimension is defined as in [Sta25, Tag 02QP]:
dimδ(Z) := δ(f(x)) + trdegκ(f(x))κ(x),
where Z ⊂X is an irreducible closed subset and x ∈Z is the generic point. Using this, we can define the
groups of cycles Zk(X, δ) of δ-dimension k and the Chow groups CHk(X, δ) for all k ∈Z as in [Sta25, Tag
02QQ] and [Sta25, Tag 02RV]. We set Z∗(X, δ) := L
k∈Z Zk(X, δ) and CH∗(X, δ) := L
k∈Z CHk(X, δ).
In later sections, we will mostly work in the situations covered by the following examples, so the
dimension function δ can be suppressed from the notation in most later applications.
Example 3.1. If S is an integral Noetherian scheme of dimension ≤1, then we can take δ(s) := dim {s}
for any s ∈S as in [Sta25, Tag 02QN]. We call this the standard dimension function. If X →S is a
closed morphism and locally of finite type, then the δ-dimension of any integral closed subscheme Z of X
is dim(Z) by [Sta25, Tag 02JX]. In this case, Zk(X) := Zk(X, δ) and CHk(X) := CHk(X, δ) coincide with
the usual notions.
Example 3.2. We fix an integral Noetherian Cohen–Macaulay scheme S of finite Krull dimension. In
this case, we always equip S with the dimension function δ defined in [Sta25, Tag 0F91], i.e.
δ(s) := −dim(OS,s).
(3.1)
By [Sta25, Tag 02JX], we have
dimδ(Z) ≥dim(Z) −dim(S),
for any morphism X →S locally of finite type and integral closed subscheme Z ⊂X, and equality holds
if X →S is closed.
Note that if dim S ≤1, δ + dim S coincides with the standard dimension function of Example 3.1. So
up to a degree shift, the different choices of δ in Example 3.1 and 3.2 do not affect any result.

3.2. Flat base change. Following [Sta25, Tag 0FVF], we recall the behavior of Chow groups under flat
base change. Let S′ be a locally Noetherian and universally catenary scheme with a dimension function
δ′. We fix a flat morphism g: S′ →S such that there exists d ∈Z with
δ′(s′) = δ(s) + d
for any s ∈S and any generic point s′ of any irreducible component of g−1(s).
Example 3.3. Assume that S is an integral Noetherian scheme of dimension ≤1 with the standard
dimension function as in Example 3.1. Let S′ be another integral Noetherian scheme of dimension ≤1
with a flat morphism g: S′ →S (not necessarily locally of finite type). Then by [Sta25, Tag 08EI], g is
dominant and the integer
d := dim S′ −dim S ∈{−1, 0, 1}
satisfies dim({s′}) = dim({s}) + d for any s ∈g(S′) and any generic point s′ of g−1({s}).

g∗: CHk(X, δ) →CHk+d(XS′, δ′).

3.5. Chern characters. We now introduce a well-behaved notion of Chern characters for bounded co-
herent complexes. We fix an integral Noetherian regular scheme S of finite Krull dimension with the
dimension function δ as in (3.1).
Recall that for a scheme X that is quasi-projective and lci over S, we define the virtual Todd class of
X over S as
td(X/S) := td(TX/S) ∈A∗(X, δ)Q.
Since X is quasi-projective and lci over S, the cotangent complex LX/S and the tangent complex TX/S
are perfect complexes. Moreover, X is quasi-compact, so td(TX/S) ∈A∗(X, δ)Q is well-defined.
It is clear that when X is smooth over S, the class td(X/S) is exactly the usual Todd class of X over
S defined by the relative tangent bundle. More generally, the class [TX/S] ∈K0(X) is the same as the
virtual tangent bundle in the sense of [Ful98, B.7.6], and td(X/S) is the same as the notion of virtual
Todd class used in [Ful98].

χ(E ⊗ωX) = −χ(E, OX) = −χ(DX(E)).
These together with part (a) imply part (b).
□
For later convenience, we define a class of morphisms as follows.
Definition 3.31. We say that a projective flat morphism f : X →S of relative dimension n ≥1 between
Noetherian schemes is admissible if it satisfies one of the following assumptions.
(1) f is lci.
(2) n = 2, each geometric fiber of f is either lci or a normal Q-Gorenstein surface, and for any
morphism D →S essentially of finite type from the spectrum D of a DVR, the total space XD
is Q-Gorenstein.
(3) n = 2, S = Spec(k) for a field k, and Xk is a normal surface.
(4) n = 3, each geometric fiber of f is a normal Q-factorial threefold that is lci in codimension 2,
and for any morphism D →S essentially of finite type from the spectrum D of a DVR, the total
space XD is Q-factorial.
By definition, being admissible is stable under base change essentially of finite type. In the following,
we give some non-trivial examples of admissible morphisms.
Example 3.32. Given a projective flat morphism f : X →S of relative dimension n ≥1 between
Noetherian schemes.
(a) If f is Gorenstein, or more generally, naively Q-Gorenstein in the sense of [LN18, Definition 7.1],
and each geometric fiber of f is lci or a normal surface, then f is admissible. Indeed, this follows
from [LN18, Lemma 7.20].
(b) If S = Spec(k) for an algebraically closed field k which is not the algebraic closure of a finite
field and X is a normal Q-factorial projective threefold over k that is lci in codimension 2, then
X →S is admissible by [BGS11, Th´eor`eme 6.5].
(c) Using [BGS11, Th´eor`eme 6.5] and [Kol23, Theorem 2.91], it follows that if S has characteristic 0
and each geometric fiber of f is a normal projective threefold with isolated Q-factorial canonical
singularities, then f is admissible.

A key property of admissible morphisms is the constancy of the top Chern character in a flat family.
Lemma 3.33. Let f : X →S be an admissible morphism and E ∈Dqc(X) be a S-perfect object. Assume
that S is Nagata and has finite Krull dimension. Then the function
s 7→chn(Es) ∈Q
is a locally constant function on S and chn(Es) = chn(Et) for any point t →S with the image s ∈S.
Proof. When f is lci, the result follows from Lemma 3.23. In the remaining cases, it is clear that χ(Es),
rk(Es), χ(OXs), and the Mumford intersection numbers are constant and compatible with field extensions.
Therefore, the last statement follows from the definition, Lemma 3.23, and Lemma 3.4.
To prove the local constancy, as in the proof of Lemma 3.23, we can base change S to the spectrum D
of a DVR that D →S is essentially of finite type by [BLM+21, Lemma 11.19].
The result for case (3) is obvious. In the case (2) in Definition 3.31, by the Q-Gorenstein assumption,
we can take m ∈Z>0 so that mch1(ω•
XD/D) is Cartier. Hence, the result follows from Lemma 3.13. In
the case (4) in Definition 3.31, by the Q-factorial assumption, we can take m ∈Z so that mch1(ω•
XD/D)
and mch1(ED) are both Cartier. Then the result also follows from Lemma 3.13.
□
We do not expect Mumford intersection numbers to be locally constant for all flat families of Weil
divisors in normal projective surfaces, and This is the reason for imposing the Q-Gorenstein condition in
the surface case of Definition 3.31.
4. Stability conditions
In this section, we recall definitions and basic properties of (weak) stability conditions on triangulated
categories. We follow [Bri07, BLM+21]. Fix a triangulated category D.
Definition 4.1. A slicing P on D is a collection {P(ϕ)}ϕ∈R of full additive subcategories of D that
satisfies
• P(ϕ + 1) = P(ϕ)[1] for all ϕ ∈R,
• HomD(P(ϕ1), P(ϕ2)) = 0 for ϕ1 > ϕ2, and
• (Harder–Narasimhan filtrations) for any non-zero E ∈D, there exists a finite sequence of mor-
phisms

such that the cone cone(si) is in P(ϕi) for a sequence ϕ1 > ϕ2 > · · · > ϕm of real numbers.
If 0 ̸= E ∈D, we set
HN−

HN+

and
ϕ+

ϕ−

For an interval I ⊂R, we define
P(I) := {E : ϕ+

where ⟨−⟩denotes the extension closure.
Definition 4.2. Let Λ be a finite rank lattice and v: K(D) →Λ be a group homomorphism.
• A weak pre-stability condition on D (with respect to Λ) is a pair σ = (Z, P) where P is a slicing of
D and Z : Λ →C is a group homomorphism, called the central charge, such that for all non-zero
objects E ∈P(ϕ), we have Z(v(E)) ∈R>0 · eiπϕ for ϕ /∈Z and Z(v(E)) ∈R≥0 · eiπϕ for ϕ ∈Z.
If, moreover, for all ϕ ∈R and non-zero objects E ∈P(ϕ), we have Z(v(E)) ̸= 0, then σ is
called a pre-stability condition.
By abuse of notation, we write Z(E) for Z(v(E)). The non-zero objects of P(ϕ) are called
σ-semistable of phase ϕ. Simple objects of P(ϕ) are called σ-stable.

• A weak pre-stability condition σ = (Z, P) satisfies the support property if there exists a quadratic
form Q on (Λ/ΛZ)R such that Q|(ker(Z)/ΛZ)R is negative definite and Q(v(E)) ≥0 for any σ-
semistable object E ∈D. Here,
ΛZ := ⟨v(E): E ∈P(0, 1], Z(E) = 0⟩⊂Λ,
where ⟨S⟩denotes the saturated sublattice generated by a subset S ⊂Λ, and v is the induced
homomorphism v: K(D) →Λ/ΛZ.
• A (weak) pre-stability condition σ = (Z, P) is called a (weak) stability condition if σ satisfies the
support property.
Remark 4.3. Given a weak stability condition σ = (Z, A), by [BMS16, Lemma 11.4], the support

|Z(v)| ≤D and Q(v) ≥0, as these lattice points are contained in a compact subset in (Λ/ΛZ)R.

with respect to v: K(D) →Λ can also be regarded as a weak stability condition with respect to v: K(D) →
Λ/ΛZ.

In the following, we recall an alternative description of stability conditions.
Definition 4.6. A stability function (resp. weak stability function) Z on an abelian category A is a
group homomorphism Z : K(A) →C such that for all non-zero E ∈A, we have Im(Z(E)) ≥0, and if
Im(Z(E)) = 0 then Re(Z(E)) < 0 (resp. Re(Z(E)) ≤0).
For any non-zero E ∈A, we define its phase by

Definition 4.7. Given any homomorphism Z : K(A) →C, we define AZ ⊂A as the subcategory of
objects E ∈A with Z(E) = 0.
It is clear from the definition that a weak stability function Z on A is a stability function if and only
if AZ = 0.
Definition 4.8. We say a weak stability function Z on an abelian category A satisfies the HN property
if every non-zero object E ∈A admits an HN filtration: a sequence

4.1. Tilting property. We now discuss the tilting property, which allows us to rotate a weak stability
condition. We follow [BLM+21, Section 14, 19].
Definition 4.10. Let B ⊂A be an abelian subcategory of an abelian category A. We say that B is a
Noetherian torsion subcategory of A if B is a Noetherian abelian category, and if there exists a torsion
pair (B, B⊥) in A.
Remark 4.11. It is straightforward to check that a Serre subcategory B ⊂A is a Noetherian torsion
subcategory if and only if for any E ∈A, there is no infinite sequence in A:
F1 ⊊· · · ⊊Fn ⊊· · · ⊊E,
where Fn ∈B for all n ∈N+.
Definition 4.12. A weak pre-stability condition σ = (A, Z) (with respect to Λ) has the tilting property
if
(t1) AZ ⊂A is a Noetherian torsion subcategory, and
(t2) for every E ∈A with µ+
σ (E) < +∞, there exists a short exact sequence
0 →E →T(E) →T(E)/E →0
in A with T(E)/E ∈AZ and HomD(AZ, T(E)[1]) = 0.

4.2. Jordan–H¨older filtrations.
Definition 4.15. Let σ = (A, Z) be a weak pre-stability condition on D. A Jordan–H¨older (JH) filtration
of a non-zero σ-semistable object E ∈A is a filtration
0 = E0 ⊂E1 ⊂E2 ⊂· · · ⊂En = E
of subobjects of E such that the factor Ei+1/Ei is σ-stable with ϕσ(Ei+1/Ei) = ϕσ(E) for each 0 ≤i ≤
n −1.
Unlike HN filtrations, the existence of JH filtrations is not part of the definition. However, it follows
from the support property, as explained below.
In the following, we will freely use the notion and properties of quasi-abelian categories and strict
subobjects/quotient objects, see [Bri07, Section 4] for an overview. A typical example of a quasi-abelian
category is P(I) for any slicing P on D and any interval I ⊂R of length < 1. We say that a quasi-
abelian category is of finite length if, for every object, every ascending chain of strict subobjects and
every descending chain of strict quotients stabilizes. In an abelian category this is equivalent to being
both Noetherian and Artinian.

JH filtrations may not be unique. If σ is a stability condition, the set of isomorphism classes of graded

5. Stability conditions in families
In this section, we collect definitions and properties needed in the relative setting. We mainly follow
[BLM+21].
5.1. Numerical K-groups. Let X be a projective scheme over a field k and D ⊂Db(X) be a strong
semi-orthogonal component. Recall that we have the Euler pairing

where Dperf := D ∩Dperf(X). When the base field is clear from the context, we will always omit it from
the notation. We define the numerical K-group of D to be the quotient of the usual K-group K(D) by
the null space of χ on the right, which is denoted by Knum(D). Similarly, we denote by Knum(Dperf)
the quotient of K(Dperf) by the null space of χ on the left. By [BLM+21, Lemma 12.7], Knum(D) and
Knum(Dperf) are finite rank lattices.
Now given a field extension k ⊂k1, the pullback functor D →Dk1 induces homomorphisms K(D) →
K(Dk1) and K(Dperf) →K(Dk1,perf). By [BLM+21, Lemma 12.14], it also induces
ηk1/k : Knum(Dperf) →Knum(Dk1,perf).
Therefore, as explained in [BLM+21, Proposition and Definition 12.15], dualizing this map gives the
following pushforward map

such that the image Knum(D)k1 := im(η∨
k1/k) contains Knum(D) as a subgroup of finite index; moreover, it
is contained in Knum(D)k.

Definition 5.7. Assume that σk = (Zk, Ak) is also a weak pre-stability condition. We say E ∈D is
geometrically σ-stable if Ek is σk-stable.
5.3. Torsion and torsion-free objects. We fix a morphism f : X →S and a semi-orthogonal compo-
nent D ⊂Db(X) satisfying Assumption 5.1. Moreover, we assume that C := S is a Dedekind scheme, i.e.
C is integral, regular, and one-dimensional.
Recall that for a Dedekind scheme C, we write p ∈C for a closed point, c ∈C for an arbitrary point,
and K for its fraction field.
The following notions of torsion and torsion-free objects relative to C are crucial.
Definition 5.8. An object E ∈D is called C-torsion if it is the pushforward of an object in DZ for a
proper closed subschemes Z ⊂C. We denote by DC-tor the subcategory of C-torsion objects in D.
According to [BLM+21, Lemma 6.4], an object E ∈D is C-torsion if and only if EK = 0. Moreover,
we have an exact triangle of triangulated categories:
DC-tor →D →DK.
Definition 5.9. Let AC ⊂D be the heart of a C-local t-structure. We say E ∈AC is C-torsion-free
if it does not contain any non-zero C-torsion subobject. We denote by AC-tor ⊂AC the subcategory of
C-torsion objects, and by AC-tf ⊂AC the subcategory of C-torsion-free objects.
The following result proved in [BLM+21, Lemma 6.6] will be useful.
Lemma 5.10. The subcategory AC-tf ⊂AC is closed under subobjects and extensions. The subcategory
AC-tor ⊂AC is closed under subobjects, quotients, and extensions.
Recall that for the heart AC ⊂D of a C-local t-structure, we have an induced heart Ac for any c ∈C
by [BLM+21, Theorem 5.6, 5.7].
Lemma 5.11 ([BLM+21, Lemma 6.12]). If E ∈AC is C-torsion-free, then Ec ∈Ac for each c ∈C.
Definition 5.12. We say AC has a C-torsion theory if the pair of subcategories (AC-tor, AC-tf) forms a
torsion pair.
Remark 5.13. By [BLM+21, Remark 6.16], if AC is Noetherian, then it has a C-torsion theory.
To check the existence of a C-torsion theory, we need the following criterion.

Lemma 5.14 ([BLM+21, Theorem 17.1]). Let AC be the heart of a C-local t-structure. Assume that
(a) AC universally satisfies openness of flatness, i.e. for every morphism of finite presentation T →S
from an affine scheme T and every T-perfect object E ∈Db(XT ), the set
{t ∈T : Et ∈At}
is open, and
(b) for every closed point p ∈C, there exists a weak stability condition σp = (Ap, Zp) so that
AZp
p
⊂Ap is a Noetherian torsion subcategory.
Then AC admits a C-torsion theory.
Proof. By [BLM+21, Theorem 17.1], it suffices to show that the condition (a) implies the property in
[BLM+21, Definition 10.4], i.e. for every morphism T →S from a scheme T and every T-perfect object
E on XT , the set
{t ∈T : Et ∈(Aqc)t}
is open. Indeed, by [BLM+21, Lemma 10.6], it suffices to check it for all morphisms of finite presentation
T →S from affine schemes. By Assumption 5.1, we know that T is Noetherian and XT →T is flat
projective. Therefore, any T-perfect object E on XT lies in Db(XT ) by Lemma 2.10. Moreover, Et ∈
Db(Xt), so we have an identification
{t ∈T : Et ∈(Aqc)t} = {t ∈T : Et ∈(Aqc)t ∩Db(Xt)}.
Now the result follows from (Aqc)t ∩Db(Xt) = At by the construction of (Aqc)t in [BLM+21, Theorem
5.3].

5.6. Flat families of fiberwise stability conditions. Now, we can define the notion of flat families of
fiberwise stability conditions. We fix a morphism f : X →S and a semi-orthogonal component D ⊂Db(X)

Definition 5.21. A flat family of fiberwise stability conditions on D over S is a collection of numerical
stability conditions σ = (σs = (Zs, Ps))s∈S on Ds for every point s ∈S such that:
(c1) σ universally has locally constant central charges, i.e. for every morphism T →S and every
T-perfect object E ∈Dqc(XT ) such that Et ∈Dt for all t ∈T, the function T →C given by
t 7→Zt(Et) is locally constant.

(c3) For any morphism C →S which is essentially of finite type from a Dedekind scheme C, the
stability condition σc for any c ∈C is induced by an HN structure σC on DC over C in the sense
of Lemma 5.17.

Definition 5.26. Let f : X →S be a flat, proper, finitely presented morphism of schemes. An S-perfect

Exti
Xs(Es, Es) = 0
for every i < 0. We denote by Dpug(X/S) ⊂Dqc(X) the full subcategory of universally gluable S-perfect

From now on, we fix a morphism f : X →S and a semi-orthogonal component D ⊂Db(X) satisfying

Definition 5.27. We denote by
Mpug(D/S): (Sch/S)op →Grpd
the functor whose value on T ∈(Sch/S) consists of all E ∈Dpug(XT /T) such that Et ∈Dt for all t ∈T.

of finite type over S and E ∈M(B) is an object such that for every geometric point ¯s over S and
E ∈M(κ(¯s)), there exists a κ(¯s)-rational point b of B ×S Spec(κ(¯s)) such that Eb ∼= E.

that ϕ(Z(v)) = ψ. We denote by

σ (v): (Sch/S)op →Grpd
the functor whose value on T ∈(Sch/S) consists of all T-perfect objects E ∈Dpug(XT /T) such that for
all t ∈T, we have Et ∈Dt, Et is geometrically σt-stable of phase ψ, and v([Et]) = v in Λ.
Similarly, we define Mσ(v) to be the corresponding moduli functor parameterizing σt-semistable of
phase ψ and class v.
Definition 5.30. Let σ be a flat family of fiberwise weak stability conditions on D over S. We say σ
satisfies boundedness (with respect to Λ) if Mst
σ (v) is bounded for every v ∈Λ.
Definition 5.31. Let σ be a flat family of fiberwise (weak) stability conditions on D over S with respect
to a relative Mukai homomorphism v: Knum(D/S) →Λ. We say σ satisfies the support property with
respect to Λ if:
(b1) There exists a quadratic form Q on (Λ/ΛZ)R such that Q|(ker(Zs)/ΛZ)R is negative definite for
every s ∈S, and for every σs-semistable object E ∈Ds, we have Q(v(E)) ≥0.
(b2) σ satisfies boundedness with respect to Λ.
In this case, we call σ a (weak) stability condition on D over S (with respect to Λ).
6. Tilt-stability on triangulated categories
In this section, we describe the construction of a family of weak stability conditions from a given weak
stability condition. The proofs in this section are rather technical, and we suggest skipping this section
on a first reading since we will only apply the results of Theorem 6.1 and 6.24 in later sections.
We fix a triangulated category D and a group homomorphism v: K(D) →Λ, where Λ is a finite rank
lattice. Let σ = (A), Z be a weak pre-stability condition on D with respect to v. Denote by µ(−) the
slope function associated with σ. Throughout this section, we denote by Hi(E) ∈A the i-th cohomology
object with respect to the heart A.
Recall that
AZ = {E ∈A: Z(E) = 0}
is the full subcategory of A consisting of objects with zero central charge. Also recall that for 0 ̸= E ∈A,
we denote by HN−
σ (E) and HN+
σ (E) the Harder–Narasimhan factors of E of minimal slope µ−(E) and
maximal slope µ+(E), respectively.

Lemma 6.2. If T ∈T b with Re Z(T) + b Im Z(T) = 0, then T ∈AZ.
Proof. Since T ∈T b, we know that T ∈A and Im Z(T) ≥0. However, if Im Z(T) > 0, then b = µ(T) ≥
µ−(T) and T /∈T b, a contradiction. Hence Im Z(T) = Re Z(T) = 0, i.e., T ∈AZ.
□

6.1. Large volume limit. The following results classify semistable objects at the large volume limit.
Fix a weak stability condition σ = (A, Z) on D with respect to Λ such that σ is defined over Q[i] and
satisfies the tilting property. Recall that for any b ∈R,
Zb := Im Z + i(−Re Z −b Im Z): Λ →C
is a weak stability function on Ab. By Lemma 4.13, we know that σb = (Ab, Zb) is a weak stability
condition on D with respect to Λ.
Lemma 6.4. Fix an object E ∈Ab with Z(E) ̸= 0. Then E is σb-semistable if and only if either
(a) Im Z(E) ≥0, and either
• E is a σ-semistable object with Im Z(E) > 0 and µ(E) > b, or
• Im Z(E) = 0 with no subobjects in AZ;
or
(b) Im Z(E) < 0 and we have a short exact sequence
0 →A[1] →E →B →0
in Ab such that A is a σ-semistable object with Im Z(A) > 0, µ(A) ≤b, and B ∈AZ, so that
HomD(AZ, E) = 0 when µ(A) < b.
Similarly, E is σb-stable if and only if either
(1) Im Z(E) ≥0, and
• E is a σ-stable object with Im Z(E) > 0 and µ(E) > b; or
• Im Z(E) = 0 with no subobjects in AZ and no proper quotient E ↠F in A with Im Z(F) =
0 and F /∈AZ;
or
(2) E satisfies (b), the object A in (b) is σ-stable with Hom(AZ, A[1]) = 0, and either µ(A) < b or
µ(A) = b and B = 0.
Proof. The statement for semistability follows from [PT19, Lemma 2.19] or [BLM+21, Lemma 14.17]. It
remains to consider the statement for stability. Let 0 →F →E →G →0 be any exact sequence of
σb-semistable objects in Ab. Then they are either in case (a) or (b).
If E is in case (a), then H−1(F) = H−1(E) = 0 and we have an exact sequence
0 →H−1(G) →F →E →H0(G) →0.
When H−1(G) ̸= 0, G is in case (b), so H−1(G) is a σ-semistable object with slope ≤b and Im Z(H−1(G)) >
0, and H0(G) ∈AZ. In particular, µ(G) ≤b. In this case, both µ(E) and µ(F) are bigger than b, hence
we cannot have µσb(E) = µσb(F) = µσb(G). So H−1(G) = 0 and we get an exact sequence
0 →F →E →G →0
of objects in A.
If E is a σ-semistable object with Im Z(E) > 0, then µσb(E) only depends on µ(E). So from the above
discussion, it is clear that E is σb-stable if and only if it is σ-stable. If Im Z(E) = 0 and has no subobjects
in AZ, then µσb(E) = 0 does not depend on E. Therefore, we see that E is σb-stable if and only if it has
no quotient F in A with Im Z(F) = 0 and Re Z(F) ̸= 0.

6.2. Noetherian property. Our goal is to define some weak stability conditions on D with hearts Ab.
To achieve this, we need to define some weak stability functions Z′ on Ab and then verify that they satisfy
the Harder–Narasimhan property and support property. Among these, verifying the Harder–Narasimhan
property is not straightforward. However, if Ab is Noetherian and the image of the imaginary part of Z′
is discrete in R, then Z′ has the Harder–Narasimhan property. In the next subsection, we will discuss the
properties of Z′, while in this subsection, we prove that under the following conditions, Ab is Noetherian,
following [PT19, BM11].
For any b ∈R, we set
Ib := {E ∈Ab : Re Z(E) + b Im Z(E) = 0}.

Lemma 6.7. Let b ∈R. Assume that
(a) the abelian category A is Noetherian, and
(b) there is no infinite sequence in A:
G1 ⊊· · · ⊊Gn ⊊· · ·
where Gn ∈Fb and Gn+1/Gn ∈AZ for any n ∈N+.
Then Ib is a Noetherian torsion subcategory of Ab.
Proof. By Lemma 6.3, Ib is a Serre subcategory of Ab. Assume that Ib is not a Noetherian torsion
subcategory. Then by Remark 4.11, there is an infinite sequence in Ab:
F1 ⊊· · · ⊊Fn ⊊· · · ⊊E,
where Re Z(Fn) + b Im Z(Fn) = 0 for any n ≥1.
Applying the cohomology functor with respect to the heart A, we obtain an infinite sequence in A:
H−1(F1) ⊆· · · ⊆H−1(Fn) ⊆· · · ⊆H−1(E).
Since A is Noetherian, this sequence stabilizes. Therefore, we may assume that
H−1(Fn) = H−1(Fn+1) ⊆H−1(E)
for any n ∈N+. Thus, by taking the cohomology long exact sequence associated to the triangle
Fn →Fn+1 →Fn+1/Fn,
we get an exact sequence in A for any n ≥1:
0 →H−1(Fn+1/Fn) →H0(Fn) →H0(Fn+1) →H0(Fn+1/Fn) →0.
(6.1)
By Lemma 6.3 and Re Z(Fn) + b Im Z(Fn) = 0, we see that H−1(Fn) is either a σ-semistable object
with slope b or H−1(Fn) = 0, and H0(Fn) ∈AZ. Combining this with (6.1), we know that the object
H−1(Fn+1/Fn) also belongs to AZ.
Therefore, we must have H−1(Fn+1/Fn) = 0 for any n ≥1 as
H−1(Fn+1/Fn) ∈Fb. This together with Lemma 6.3 implies Fn+1/Fn ∈AZ.
For any n ≥1, let En be the cokernel of Fn →E in Ab.
Then we have an infinite sequence of
epimorphisms in Ab:
E ↠E1 ↠· · · ↠En ↠· · ·
with ker(En ↠En+1) ∼= Fn+1/Fn. Similarly, after taking the cohomology functor with respect to the
heart A, we obtain an infinite sequence in A:
H0(E) ↠H0(E1) ↠· · · ↠H0(En) ↠· · · .
Because A is Noetherian, the above sequence stabilizes. Thus, after discarding finitely many terms, we
may also assume that H0(En) = H0(En+1). Combining this with H−1(Fn+1/Fn) = 0, we get an exact
sequence
0 →H−1(En) →H−1(En+1) →Fn+1/Fn →0
in A for each n ≥1.
Now, we set Gn := H−1(En) ∈Fb. We then get an infinite sequence in A:
G1 ⊆· · · ⊆Gn ⊆· · ·
where Gn ∈Fb and Gn+1/Gn ∼= Fn+1/Fn ∈AZ \ {0} for any n ≥1.
This contradicts our second
assumption on A. Thus, Ib is a Noetherian torsion subcategory of Ab.
□
Corollary 6.8. Let b ∈R and we assume that
(a) the set {Re Z(E) + b Im Z(E) ∈R: E ∈Ab} is discrete in R,
(b) the abelian category A is Noetherian, and
(c) there is no infinite sequence in A:
G1 ⊊· · · ⊊Gn ⊊· · ·
where Gn ∈Fb and Gn+1/Gn ∈AZ for any n ∈N+.

6.5. Skewed weak stability functions. In this subsection, we review necessary definitions and results
from [Bri07]. We will freely use the notions and properties of quasi-abelian categories, strict subobjects,
strict quotient objects, and strict morphisms, see [Bri07, Section 4] for an overview. A typical example of
a quasi-abelian category is P(I) for any slicing P on D and any interval I ⊂R of length < 1.
Definition 6.12. Let B ⊂D be a quasi-abelian subcategory and fix α ∈R. A skewed weak stability
function on B with phase interval (α, α + 1] is a group homomorphism
P : K(B) →C
such that for any 0 ̸= E ∈B, we have
P(E) ∈R>0 · exp(iπψ),
α < ψ ≤α + 1
if ψ /∈Z and
P(E) ∈R≥0 · exp(iπψ),
α < ψ ≤α + 1
if ψ ∈Z. The number ψ = ψ(E) is called the phase of E.
We say E is P-semistable if ψ(F) ≤ψ(E) ≤ψ(E/F) for any non-zero strict proper subobject F ,→E.
An HN filtration of 0 ̸= E ∈B with respect to P is a filtration of strict subobjects
0 = E0 ⊂E1 ⊂E2 ⊂· · · ⊂Em = E
so that Ei/Ei−1 is P-semistable with ψ(E1/E0) > ψ(E2/E1) > · · · > ψ(Em/Em−1).
We will apply the theory of skewed weak stability functions in the following setting. Fix a weak stability
condition σ = (P, Z) on D. Fix a number 0 < ϵ0 < 1
8 and a group homomorphism P : K(D) →C such
that
|P(E) −Z(E)| ≤sin(πϵ0)|Z(E)|
for any σ-semistable object E /∈AZ and Z(F) = 0 implies P(F) = 0 for any F ∈A.
Lemma 6.13 ([Bri07, Lemma 7.3]). Assume that E ∈P(a, b) is P-semistable with 0 < b −a < 1 −2ϵ0,
then E ∈P(ψ(E) −ϵ0, ψ(E) + ϵ0).
Definition 6.14. Suppose 0 < b −a < 1 −2ϵ0. A non-zero object E ∈P(a, b) is said to be enveloped by
P(a, b) if a + ϵ0 ≤ψ(E) ≤b −ϵ0.
A thin subcategory of D is a full subcategory of the form P(a, b) where 0 < b −a < 1 −2ϵ0.
The following property is important in our later proof.
Lemma 6.15 ([Bri07, Lemma 7.5]). Suppose an object 0 ̸= E ∈D is enveloped by both thin categories
P(a, b) and P(a′, b′). Then E is P-semistable in P(a, b) if and only if E is P-semistable in P(a′, b′).
Definition 6.16. A maximal destabilizing quotient (mdq) of an object 0 ̸= E ∈P(a, b) is a non-zero
strict quotient E ↠B in P(a, b) such that any non-zero strict quotient E ↠B′ satisfies ψ(B′) ≥ψ(B),
with equality only if E ↠B′ factors via E ↠B.

8. Bogomolov–Gieseker inequalities for semistable sheaves
In this section, we first introduce the Le Potier function in the absolute and relative setting. Then we
prove Theorem 8.4, which gives a quadratic upper bound of (relative) Le Potier function. We end this
section with a sharper inequality on normal varieties (cf. Theorem 8.6).
8.1. Bogomolov–Gieseker functions. One of the most important properties of slope-stability on smooth
projective varieties is the Bogomolov–Gieseker (BG) inequality (cf. [HL10a, Lan04]). Therefore, we intro-
duce the following notions.

Now we can prove a version of Theorem 8.6 using ∆(−).
Proposition 8.15. Let X be a normal projective variety of dimension n ≥2 over an algebraically closed
field k which is lci in codimension 2. Then for any ample line bundle H, there exists a constant CH ≥0
such that CH-BG inequality holds for H. If char(k) = 0 and X has rational singularities, then we can
take CH = 0.
Proof. The proof is motivated by [Lan22, Theorem 3.4]. When n = 2, this is Lemma 8.10. Now, assume
that n ≥3 and the statement holds in dimension ≤n −1.
Up to replacing H with a multiple of itself, we can assume that H is very ample. We claim that we
can take CH := CH|T , where T is a general normal integral complete intersection surface by divisors in
|H|. Note that such T always exists by [FOV99, Corollary 3.4.14]. If char(k) = 0 and X has rational
singularities, then T also has rational singularities by [KM98, Theorem 5.42], and we have CH = CH|T = 0
by Lemma 8.10. Note that CH is independent of the choice of general T and only depends on |H| by a
similar argument in [Kos23b]. Indeed, for a family X1 →C of divisors in |H| over a smooth curve C,
we can first take a resolution X2 of X1 by [CP19], which is still flat over C and also resolves the generic
fiber of X1 →C. After shrinking the base curve without changing the generic fiber, we may assume
that X2 →C is smooth projective. Then the argument in [Kos23b, Definition-Proposition 4.1] applies
verbatim.
We prove by induction on the rank of µH-semistable sheaves. Let E be a µH-semistable sheaf of rank r
on X. By Lemma 3.24 and 7.6, we may assume that E is reflexive. When r = 1, note that its restriction
to a general divisor in |H| remains semistable, and the result follows from the fact that the restriction does
not change the value of the H-discriminant by Lemma 3.16(b) and Theorem 3.25(e). Now, we assume
that r > 1 and the result holds for any µH-semistable sheaf of rank < r.
Let L ⊂|H| be a general pencil. Let q: Y →X be the blow-up of the base locus of L and p: Y →L ∼= P1
is the natural projection, which is the restriction of the universal family over |H| to L. Therefore, Y is
also a normal projective variety over k which is lci in codimension 2, q is lci by Lemma 8.12, and p is flat
projective. Moreover, q∗E = Lq∗E is torsion-free by Lemma 8.13 and E′ := (q∗E)∨∨is a reflexive sheaf
on Y of rank r with q∗E′ ⊂E by Lemma 8.12.
Let 0 = E0 ⊂E1 ⊂· · · ⊂Em = E′ be the relative HN filtration with respect to the collection
(p∗OL(1), q∗H, . . . , q∗H) and p. We can assume that m > 1, otherwise E′|p−1(b) is µH|D-semistable for
a general point b = [D] ∈L, and the result follows from Lemma 3.21(c) and the induction hypothesis
on dimensions. As explained in [Lan04, 3.9], it is also the (absolute) HN filtration with respect to the
collection (p∗OL(1), q∗H, . . . , q∗H). Let Fi := Ei/Ei−1, then it is a (p∗OL(1), q∗H, . . . , q∗H)-semistable
torsion-free sheaf on Y of rank r > ri > 0 with the (p∗OL(1), q∗H, . . . , q∗H)-slope µi. Moreover, (Fi)|D
is µH|D-semistable for a general divisor in L as it is a factor of the relative HN filtration.
By [Ful98, Proposition 6.7(e)], we can write ch1(Fi) = q∗Mi + biN, where Mi is a Weil divisor on X,
N is the exceptional divisor of q, and bi ∈Z. Note that P

Lemma 9.3. Fix an object E ∈Ab(X). Then E is σb-semistable if and only if either
(a) v0(E) ≥0, and E is a torsion-free µ-semistable sheaf with slope > b or a torsion sheaf that is
either pure of codimension one or of codimension at least 2; or
(b) v0(E) < 0 and we have a short exact sequence
0 →A[1] →E →B →0
in Ab(X) such that A is a torsion-free µ-semistable sheaf with slope ≤b and B is a torsion sheaf
supported in codimension at least 2; when µ(A) < b, HomX(T, E) = 0 for every sheaf T supported
in codimension ≥2.
Similarly, E is (geometrically) σb-stable if and only if either
(1) v0(E) ≥0, and
• E is a torsion-free (geometrically) µ-stable sheaf with slope > b; or
• E is a pure torsion sheaf of rank one on its scheme-theoretic support, which is (geometri-
cally) integral of codimension one or of dimension zero; or
(2) E satisfies (b), the sheaf A in (b) is a torsion-free S2 (geometrically) µ-stable sheaf, and either
µ(A) < b and the sequence is non-split, or µ(A) = b and B = 0.
Proof. By base change to k, the result follows from Lemma 6.4.
□
As an immediate application of the above classification, we have:

σb-stable if and only if Ek1 is geometrically σb
k1-stable.
Proof. Note that Db(X) →Db(Xk1) is a faithful functor. Since the rank, torsion-freeness, S2 property,
geometric µ-stability, and geometric integrality are preserved after base change to k1, the result follows
from Lemma 9.3.
□

following, we collect some useful results for tilt-stability. The next two lemmas describe the behavior of

mension at least 2.

10. Bayer–Macr`ı–Toda Conjecture
We now focus on threefolds. We first discuss conjectural ch3-inequalities in Section 10.1, generalizing
the formulations in [BMT14, BMS16]. Next, Theorem 10.7 shows that these inequalities can be used to
construct stability conditions. Finally, the behavior of ch3-inequalities under degeneration is investigated
in Theorem 10.12.
Throughout this section, we follow the simplification of the notation as in Section 9. We fix a triple
(X, H, γ) as in Setup 7.1 that has the standard BG function with n = 3 such that either
(1) d = 3, i.e. X is lci, or
(2) Xk is normal integral Q-factorial and is lci in codimension 2.

10.3. BMT Conjecture via degeneration. We end this section with one of our main theorems (cf. The-
orem 10.12). The main idea is to use a semistable reduction argument as in [BLM+21]. However, since we
work with weak HN structures, the restriction of a semistable object to the special fiber need not remain
semistable (cf. [BLM+21, Lemma 15.7]). To overcome this issue, we need the following lemma, which says
that the restriction to the special fiber preserves semistability up to modifications.
Theorem 10.10 (Semistable reduction of tilt-stability). Let (f : X →S, L, γ) as in Setup 7.7 with f
Cohen–Macaulay, n ≤3, d ≥2, and Bγ = 0. Fix (b, w) ∈U γ
X/S,L. Assume furthermore C →S is
a morphism essentially of finite type from the spectrum C of a DVR with the fraction field K and the
closed point p ∈C. If EK ∈Ab(XK) is a νb,w-semistable object with vb
1(EK) ̸= 0, then we can find a
σb,w

11. Singular Calabi–Yau/Fano threefolds
In this section, we first reduce Conjecture 10.2 to a simple inequality for ch2 (cf. Theorem 11.5).
Then we apply it to prove Conjecture 10.2 for many Fano threefolds (cf. Corollary 11.9) and Calabi–Yau
threefolds (cf. Section 11.3). In particular, by Theorem 10.7, there exists an explicit family of stability
conditions on their derived categories.

(1) X is lci, or
(2) Xk is normal integral Q-factorial Gorenstein4 and is lci in codimension 2.

11.2. Singular Fano threefolds. In the rest of this section, we assume that the base field k is alge-
braically closed of characteristic zero.
A Fano threefold X is a normal projective 3-dimensional variety over k with rational Gorenstein singu-
larities and −KX ample. Thus, the classical BG inequality holds for X by Theorem 8.6. In this case, by
[PS99, Proposition 2.1.2], Pic(X) is always a finite rank lattice. We define its index ι(X), degree d(X),
and genus g(X) by
ι(X) := max

0 ̸= i ∈Z: 1
i KX ∈Pic(X)

,
d(X) := (−KX)3
ι(X)3 ,
and
g(X) := 1
2(−KX)3 + 1.
We set
H := −
1
ι(X)KX.
If Pic(X) has rank one, we call it a prime Fano threefold. In this case, H is the unique ample generator
of Pic(X). By [PS99], for a Fano threefold X, we have 1 ≤ι(X) ≤4. Moreover, ι(X) = 4 if and only if
X ∼= P3, and ι(X) = 3 if and only if X ⊂P4 is a quadric threefold.
Now, we want to apply Theorem 11.5 to Fano threefolds. To this end, we need an easy lemma.

12. Kuznetsov components of singular Fano threefolds
In this section, we focus on Kuznetsov components of singular Fano threefolds. As another application
of our framework, we construct stability conditions on Kuznetsov components of certain singular Fano
threefolds. Moreover, its relative version proves a singular analog of [KS25, Conjecture 1.8] (cf. Corollary
12.10).
12.1. Relative exceptional collections. We first review the notion of relative exceptional collections.
Definition 12.1. Let f : X →S be a proper flat morphism between Noetherian schemes. We say an
object E ∈Dperf(X) is a relative exceptional object in Db(X) over S if
Rf∗RHomX(E, E) ∼= OS .
A relative exceptional collection in Db(X) over S is a sequence E1, · · · , Em of relative exceptional objects
in Db(X) over S such that Rf∗RHomX(Ei, Ej) ∼= 0 for all i > j.
By [BLM+21, Lemma 3.22], a sequence E1, · · · , Em ∈Dperf(X) is a relative exceptional collection in
Db(X) over S if and only if (E1)s, · · · , (Em)s ∈Dperf(Xs) is an exceptional collection in Db(Xs) for every
point s ∈S, or equivalently for every closed point s ∈S.
We first recall the following result from [BLM+21].
Lemma 12.2 ([BLM+21, Lemma 3.23]). Let f : X →S be a proper morphism of finite Tor-dimension
between Noetherian schemes. If E ∈Dperf(X) is a relative exceptional object in Db(X) over S, then there
is a fully faithful functor
αE : Db(S) →Db(X),
F 7→Lf ∗F ⊗L E,
which has a right adjoint. Moreover, if ω•
f is invertible, then αE has a left adjoint.
Using the properties of dualizing complexes (cf. Lemma 2.13), an argument similar to that of [BLM+21,
Lemma 3.25] gives:
Lemma 12.3. Let f : X →S be a proper morphism of finite Tor-dimension between Noetherian schemes
and E1, · · · , Em ∈Dperf(X) be a relative exceptional collection. Then there is an S-linear semi-orthogonal
decomposition of finite cohomological amplitude
Db(X) = ⟨D1, αE1(Db(S)), · · · , αEm(Db(S))⟩
such that the inclusion D1 ,→Db(X) has a left adjoint.
Furthermore, if f is Gorenstein and S is regular, then all components above are admissible; in partic-
ular, the decomposition above is strong.

ω•

Corollary 7.6] and [BLMS23, Proposition 5.1] by Proposition 12.4 and Proposition 12.5, respectively.
□

Db(X) = ⟨RX/S, αOX(Db(S))⟩

Finally, we discuss the index 1 case. For an index 1 prime Fano threefold X, when g(X) is even, we
say a rank 2 vector bundle UX is a Mukai bundle if it is µ−KX-stable and satisfies
• det(UX) ∼= OX(KX),
• Hi(X, UX) = 0 for all i, and
• RHomX(UX, UX) = k.
In particular, OX, U∨
X is an exceptional collection in Db(X). When X is factorial, terminal, and g(X) ≥6,
the existence and the uniqueness of a Mukai bundle is known (cf. [BKM24, BKM25]).
In the case of index 1 Fano threefolds, we have the following.
Theorem 12.9. Let f : X →S be an admissible morphism to a regular Nagata scheme S of finite Krull
dimension which is quasi-projective over a Noetherian affine scheme of characteristic zero. Assume that
for each geometric point t →S, Xt is an index 1 prime Fano threefold of even genus g ≥6 and there exists
a vector bundle UX on X such that UXt is a Mukai bundle. Assume furthermore that f has a smooth
fiber. Then we have an S-linear strong semi-orthogonal decomposition of finite cohomological amplitude
Db(X) = ⟨AX/S, αOX(Db(S)), αU∨

such that there exists a stability condition on AX/S over S.

As a corollary, we have the following singular analog of [KS25, Conjecture 1.8]. Recall that for each
1 ≤d ≤5, there exists a morphism X →B between varieties over k constructed in [KS25, Theorem 3.6]
such that B is a smooth curve, the total space X is smooth, Xo is a 1-nodal index 1 prime Fano threefold
of genus 2d + 2 for a closed point o ∈B, and Xb is a smooth index 1 prime Fano threefold of genus 2d + 2
for each b ∈B \ o. Moreover, there exists a vector bundle UX on X such that UXb is a Mukai bundle on
Xb for each b ∈B and we have a B-linear semi-orthogonal decomposition
Db(X) = ⟨io∗PXo, ¯
AX , αOX (Db(B)), αU∨
X (Db(B))⟩
such that PXo is a P∞,2 object on the central fiber Xo and io∗: Xo ,→X is the inclusion. Moreover, ¯

is smooth and proper over B.
In [KS25, Conjecture 1.8], it is expected that there exists a stability condition on ¯
AX over B, which
then may lead to applications of [KS25, Theorem 3.6] in studying moduli spaces. When B is the spectrum
of a complete DVR, this is proved by [LMP+26]. Applying Theorem 12.9 to this setting, we obtain an
analog of [KS25, Conjecture 1.8] by replacing ¯
AX with
AX/B = ⟨io∗PXo, ¯
AX ⟩.
Corollary 12.10. In the setting above, if d ≥2, then there exists a stability condition on AX/B over B.
Proof. Since each geometric fiber of X →B is either smooth or has at most one node, it has terminal
Gorenstein lci singularities. In particular, X →B is lci and Theorem 12.9 applies.
□
Appendix A. An alternative approach to tilt-stability
In this appendix, we discuss another construction of tilt-stability on an arbitrary projective scheme.

## Page 110


110
ZHIYU LIU AND TIANLE MAO
In particular, when dim X = 2, this approach yields a family of stability conditions on Db(X). Further-
more, the relative results established in Section 9 extend naturally to this setting. Notably, the central
charges defined via this method are automatically locally constant in families, thereby obviating the need
to verify the admissibility of morphisms.
However, the Bogomolov-Gieseker-type inequalities in this setting are not sharp for line bundles, even
in the smooth case, unless KX is proportional to H. Because this sharpness is essential for investigating
the BMT conjecture, we do not pursue this approach in the main text of the paper.
